Impact of smart energy on a three-echelon closed-loop supply chain management with reduction of carbon emissions
Authors: Nilkamal Bar, Isha Sangal, Biswajit Sarkar
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
The effective management of energy consumption and the reduction of emissions are crucial to sustainable supply chain management. Sustainability can be approached in a new way through smart energy. Smart energy combines traditional and renewable energy sources through a smart system that is more efficient than conventional energy for sustainability. The model creates a closed-loop supply chain management for tires production to find the efficiency of smart energy utilization and the remanufacturing of end-of-life tires to achieve the sustainability goal. The model determines the policy for using smart energy by finding the ratio of renewable to traditional energy. The players include a manufacturer, multiple retailers, and third-party logistics that consume energy for production and management. The manufacturer follows a manufacturing-remanufacturing strategy to produce new and remanufactured tires and supplies those tires to all retailers by following an optimal delivery schedule. The formulated profit function of the developed model is a mixed-integer nonlinear function. A classical optimization method is used to solve the model. The most important finding is that using 42% smart energy increases total profit by 12.71% and pulls down emissions by 41.98%. A mixed production strategy with variable remanufacturing earns 13.91% more profit.

Introduction

Developing a sustainable closed-loop supply chain management (CLSCM) that incorporates economic and environmental considerations is a complex and demanding task that requires a multidisciplinary approach. The fundamental aim of such a supply chain (SC) is to reduce ecological effect and maintain economic sustainability1,2. Therefore, the present study develops a CLSCM for tires to reduce ecological impacts through remanufacturing end-of-life (EOL) tires, as 1 billion waste tires are generated worldwide each year3. In 2023, Ahmed et al.4 reported that about 3 billion new tires were produced globally. In the tire manufacturing industry, energy is a basic need for tire production. Generally, manufacturing industries use energy for production, lighting, and other management services and consume most of the global energy use, which is expected to grow by 1.5% per year [Industrial Energy Consumption, Global Markets. https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/engineering/industrial-energy-consumption, 2014 (accessed 30th April 2024)]. However, the energy demand is increasing along with the increasing production of tires. Though the energy audit method can minimize consumption by quantifying energy use and waste in a plant, the main concerns about energy sources are non-renewable5.

It is well known that the price of a product increases with demand when there is a finite stock. This fact is similar to that of non-renewable energy (NRNE), as the supply of traditional energy sources is finite. Since energy costs are among the leading costs in the tire manufacturing industry, an increase in unit energy costs lowers the profit of the closed-loop tire supply chain management (CTSCM). Rather than economic aspects, non-renewable energy adversely impacts the environment. Energy usage (electricity and heat) by manufacturing companies globally contributes to 24.2% of global emissions6. Emissions from energy use can be lowered using renewable energy sources rather than NRNE. The International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA) estimates that about 29% of global electricity is generated from renewable energy sources. Therefore, it is impossible to supply cent percent renewable energy to the tire industry in the present scenario.Fig. 1Graphical presentation for the study consisting of manufacturer, retailer, and third-party logistics.

Graphical presentation for the study consisting of manufacturer, retailer, and third-party logistics.

Smart energy will be a proper substitute for NRNE. In the electricity field, according to Lund et al.7, a smart electricity grid should be considered part of a smart energy system. Smart grid (SG) is a new era of traditional power grid that employs many devices such as computers, sensors, various forms of communication technology, and data analysis techniques to connect consumers and suppliers via bidirectional communication while improving system efficiency, reliability, security, flexibility, and safety8. The smart grid integrates renewable and NRNE energy sources to meet the CTSCM’s energy demand. The combined energy supplied through a smart electricity grid is known as smart energy. A smart meter, a component of a smart energy system, provides real-time data on the amount of renewable and NRNE energy consumption. Smart energy is cost-effective and reliable, and it could decarbonize extensively, reducing emissions from the use of renewable energy. The primary purpose of the research is to make the CTSCM sustainable by making it economically viable, reducing emissions, and conserving resources through remanufacturing and renewable sources. Smart energy use by the CTSCM players is depicted in Fig. 1.

Another concern is about the EOL tire. As the demand for new tires increases, so does the quantity and range of EOL tires. According to Formela3, about 1 billion scrap tires are generated globally per year, and this could reach 1.2 billion by 2030. In particular, in India, about 1.5 million tons of used tires are generated each year, of which only 0.45 million tons are recycled. Unfortunately, these EOL tires are disposed of in the environment without proper treatment, threatening the environment and human health. Thus, one of the most challenging tasks is properly reusing or recovering end-of-life tires. Among different recovery options for EOL tires, such as reuse, recycling, and remanufacturing, remanufacturing is most popular for its quality and price.

The price of a remanufactured tire is almost 40%-50% of a brand-new tire, with quality comparable to a new one, which is both economically and socially beneficial9. Remanufacturing waste tires with an investment in the improvement of the finished item’s characteristics is a process of implementing corporate social responsibility (CSR), which increases demand and maintains a good company image10. Thus, CLSCM for tires supports social and environmental development, as well as economic development. In this study, the potential impact of smart energy on a three-echelon CLSCM is examined, with a specific focus on reducing carbon emissions. In addition to the smart energy policy used, the pricing policy for new and remanufactured tires has a crucial effect on the demand for final goods. The study uses a price-dependent quadratic demand model to examine the impact of price on demand, holding the selling price constant across new and remanufactured tires.

Orientation of the paper

The proposed model contains a literature survey in Literature review section. Problem definition, notation, and assumptions section describes the study’s problem, the essential assumptions for formulating the model, and the notation for parameters and decision variables. The mathematical model is formulated by describing different costs for each player in the supply chain management (SCM) in Mathematical expression of the model section. Following this, Solution methodology section represents the solution methodology of the problem, and Numerical experiment section is formulated to clarify some numerical examples. Discussions section discusses the results and compares them with the existing study. In Sensitivity analysis section, a sensitivity analysis of crucial parameters is presented, while in Managerial insights section, recommendations are given to the manager. Finally, conclusions to the related study and future model extension are included in Conclusions section.

Literature review

This part of the model recite a patulous survey on literature to find out significant contribution in the field and explores the research gap for the devolvement of environment and economy in the mentioned CLSCM.

Energy usage within an SCM

In SCM, the motive for energy use is two-fold: the first is production, storage, and building management, and the second is inventory movement from one echelon to another. With the Industrial Revolution, energy demand in manufacturing industries has increased. Iqbal et al.11 investigated a three-layer (primary, secondary, and closed-loop supply chain) green supply chain management (GSCM) for packaging food products. They designed their GSCM model to minimize waste and energy usage during production. They proposed in their study that energy usage might be reduced by optimizing the manufacturing cycle. As with the manufacturing cycle, the manufacturing rate is crucial to energy consumption. A serial production line model was studies by Cui et al.12 to reduce energy consumption during production. They suggested an N-policy-based constrained model of the energy-efficient control (CMEC) technique to reduce energy usage while preserving the required production rate. However, a production system can not always be perfect. When a machine fails, a significant amount of energy is lost due to frequent reboots of the production process. In this direction, Sarkar et al.5 proposed a sustainable smart production model for biofuel under a flexible manufacturing system. They aimed to produce pure biofuel that consumed less energy and to maximize total profit through a dual-channel selling strategy. They suggested energy consumption may be minimized by controlling the production rate of the flexible production system. All the above authors have discussed how to reduce energy consumption. However, the current situation demands that renewable energy be deployed as much as possible in place of NRNE.

In such a situation, Nadel and Geller13 introduced the concept of smart energy policies, in which they studied energy problems to reduce pollution by promoting a clean, efficient combined heat and power system. The main goal of smart energy Lund et al.7 showed how flexible CHP production improves energy efficiency and why a smart electricity grid is included in smart energy systems for electricity balancing problems. A power SC network was designed by Tsao and Vu14 with two players: one supplier and consumers, with the help of a smart grid. End consumers participate in generating renewable energy in their model. They used pricing and a buy-back mechanism, allowing clients to sell or purchase energy as needed. This approach helps to lessen reliance on NRNE. Calise et al.15 developed a smart energy system for residential buildings, defining smart energy as the combination of heat and power generated from solar photovoltaic and electric sources. They minimized the environmental effects of their study. Thomas and Mishra16 addressed a green energy SCM to fix the problem of energy demand. In their paper, they introduced the concept of a smart grid and explored how it integrates renewable energy with the NRNE power supply. Dai et al.17 detailed how the smart grid incorporates green technologies and environmental factors in his proposal. Finally, they emphasized the importance of internet of things (IoT) in smart grids or smart energy systems. The research trend on smart energy and sustainable SCM is given in Fig. 2a.

However, in the above discussion on smart energy and SCM, it is found that researchers either form an energy SC using a smart grid that integrates renewable energy and NRNE or apply smart energy to formulate a smart city. According to Cui et al.12, the manufacturing sector consumes around 42% of global energy consumption. Therefore, there is a research gap regarding how industries can utilize smart energy to lower energy consumption costs and emissions for production and inventory management within an SC. As per the authors’ knowledge, this is the first study to formulate a CLSCM for tires to enable SCM players to effectively use smart energy in production and inventory management to optimize total profit and minimize emissions. The next section provides a detailed survey of carbon emissions from energy consumption and transportation.

Carbon emissions for energy consumption and transportation

The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) of the United States identified that transportation and energy usage (industrial and electrical) account for the majority of emissions (Fig. 2b).Fig. 2The graphical representation displays the keyword based literature and emissions from different sectors.

The graphical representation displays the keyword based literature and emissions from different sectors.

Rahman et al.18 analyzed the impact of household consumption-based emissions on sustainable development goals in SAARC nations, indicating that increases in carbon footprints pose risks to economic and environmental development. They suggested that mitigating emissions not only supports ecological development but also sustains economic growth. An economic production quantity (EPQ) model was created by Ruidas et al.19 in response to various carbon policies to reduce emissions due to production, rework, and inventory management. They used the particle swarm optimization approach to solve their problem and verified the model under different carbon policies to find the most effective policy for emissions reduction. However, they did not consider emissions for energy consumption and transportation purposes. A review article on the dairy SC was presented by Malliaroudaki et al.20 for net zero emission by energy management. They highlighted that energy consumption is responsible for a greater share of emissions in the dairy industry. Using alternative energy sources may help achieve the net-zero carbon goal. A two-echelon SCM was proposed by Marchi et al.21 within a flexible production system to minimize carbon emissions from energy use and other purposes. They recommended a vendor-managed inventory (VMI) strategy under consignment stock to minimize energy consumption and reduce emissions by reducing machine failures and faulty products. Tian et al.22 formulated an aluminium SC for the reduction of environmental impact by lowering energy consumption in the aluminium industry. They analyzed four scenarios and discovered that energy consumption and emissions will be significantly reduced by 2030 under the business-as-usual (BAU) scenario. Mashud et al.23 developed a sustainable warehousing model to find a balance among reduction in ordering cost, deterioration, and emissions reduction. They maximized total profit by minimizing emissions from lighting, heating, air conditioning, and product deterioration under the carbon tax policy. In another study, Mashud et al.24 developed an inventory model to minimize transportation-related emissions. They considered fixed and variable transportation emissions, with variable emissions depending on fuel consumption.

De and Giri25 constructed a multi-echelon CLSCM to reduce environmental impact due to transportation under heterogeneous vehicle presence. They identified the conflict between transportation costs and emissions costs and provided insight into how proper scheduling of goods transport effectively reduces emissions. Sarkar et al.26 developed a sustainable production-inventory model for a faulty production system that considers emissions from production operations. They used green investment to reduce emissions. An automated production system was discussed for production with a flexible production system by Sarkar et al.27. They minimized total cost of the production system but without consideration of SCM. Ullah28 defined a CLSCM under a manufacturing-remanufacturing scheme with the effect of carbon emissions from transportation. They used fixed and variable carbon emissions from transportation and implemented a carbon tax to reduce emissions. Yadav et al.29 developed research by considering both fixed and variable emissions from transportation in the forward and reverse flow of goods. The fixed emissions are proportional to the fuel consumed while an empty truck moves. Variable emissions are proportional to the fuel consumed while the truck is loaded. It is variable because demand and return are variable. Hasan et al.30 developed an inventory model to minimize emissions from the retail store under an optimum investment for green technology. They investigated their model under carbon tax, cap-and-trade, and carbon offset policies and found that the carbon cap-and-trade policy efficiently reduces emissions. Zhang et al.31 investigated a smart production system (SPS) for renewable energy use and tracking technology to provide real-time information about goods. They found that efficient renewable energy use can reduce significant costs and emissions associated with the system’s energy use. Edalatpour et al.32 investigated a global sustainable SCM (GSSCM), introducing International Commercial Terms and international transportation modes, which filled the gaps with sustainability criteria in GSSCM. They considered a carbon tax policy to effectively reduce emissions and introduced waste product recycling to lessen environmental degradation. In other study, Edalatpour et al.33 evaluated an integrated model that harmonizes project management and SC decisions to optimize profit and environmental performance in sustainable construction. They conducted a case study and explained how coordination and procurement strategies reduce emissions and resource waste, thereby improving decision-making. The research trend on carbon emissions, energy consumption, and transportation is given in Fig. 2c.

However, there is no single research article that was formulated to reduce emissions from energy consumption for production, management, and transportation simultaneously in a three-echelon CLSCM. The proposed study is formulated to find how to reduce emissions for energy consumption and transportation in a three-echelon CLSCM context. Another benefit of a CLSCM is that it reduces waste by remanufacturing EOL tires. The following section describes the literature on the efficacy of remanufacturing in a flexible production and remanufacturing-based CLSCM.

Sustainability of a manufacturing and remanufacturing enabled CLSCM

An SCM describes the coordination among the SCM players linked by inventory and monetary flow. An SCM can be classified by the number of participants, such as two-, three-, or multi-echelon SCM. In a forward SC, final products are sold to the market through one or more players that follow the take-make-dispose policy. Here is the importance of closing the loop by introducing a reverse flow of EOL products for remanufacturing/reuse/recycling purposes, which reduces environmental issues34. A CLSCM helps the system toward sustainability by integrating economic, ecological, and social problems through remanufacturing, the use of renewable sources, and the creation of jobs for society35. A collaborative research article by Yang et al.36 discussed a CLSCM model in the presence of multiple retailers and a manufacturer. They evaluated the consequences of deterioration and price-dependent demand for electronic devices such as mobile phones and IC chips. Environmental concerns were addressed by Subulan et al.37 through the creation of a tire CLSC network that offers multiple recovery opportunities. They solved their model using fuzzy goal programming to address the uncertainties in manufacturing and remanufacturing tires. Taleizadeh et al.38 defined a three-tier SCM with two scenarios: single-channel forward SCM and dual forward and return channels. They employed a game-theoretic technique to solve their model, which included a specific return rate and an old-fashioned production framework. A multi-tier reverse SC model was proposed by Edalatpour et al.39 for waste management. They considered a stochastic collection rate for dry and wet waste from different collection centers, and, after separation of waste depending on its condition, they considered recycling, remanufacturing, and disposal or landfilling. They maximize the profit from reverse SCM by minimizing waste, thereby enhancing environmental sustainability.

Singhal et al.40 described remanufacturing as a critical way to achieve sustainability for electrical and electronics SCM. They described 15 essential factors and their interrelationships to implement remanufacturing successively in the Indian context using Interpretive Structural Modelling. A multi-objective mixed-integer non-liner programming (MINLP) was formulated by Abdolazimi et al.41 to minimize environmental impacts and total costs, and to maximize on-time delivery of goods. They applied their problem to the tire manufacturing industry. A systematic literature review by Akram et al.42 examined sustainability issues in a food manufacturing SC. They highlighted that IoT and blockchain effectively enhance sustainability in food manufacturing SCM. Soleimani et al.43 developed a sustainable CLSCM that minimizes energy consumption by the players involved in the CLSCM. They addressed economic sustainability by maximizing total profit, environmental sustainability by reducing emissions and energy consumption, and social sustainability by creating jobs for society. However, they did not consider emissions from energy consumption and did not address the energy types used by the SCM players. Furthermore, considering fixed demand throughout the SCM limits the model. Several researchers researched tires recovery possibilities, such as Fard et al.44 defined their model under uncertainty and solved it using genetic algorithm (GA), and Ahmed et al.4 defined their model to identify the importance of the supplier and solved it using the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon$$\end{document}-constraint approach. Researchers introduced remanufacturing/recycling to reduce environmental sustainability. However, no study explained the sustainable issues under the consideration of flexible manufacturing-remanufacturing in a CLSCM context. The research trend on manufacturing, remanufacturing, CLSCM, and sustainability is given in Fig. 2d.

However, as far as the authors know, no research article on tire manufacturing considers variable production and remanufacturing systems, which contributes to the literature on the efficiency of smart From the above discussion of the literature and Table 1, one can observe changes in research concepts with the inventions. Several researchers have introduced renewable energy concepts into SCM to reduce dependence on NRNE energy. Manufacturing-remanufacturing-based hybrid models are introduced in the literature for socio-economic benefits. However, most models were optimized under fixed production and remanufacturing rates and fixed demand rates55. Several studies have examined emissions issues in production and transportation to reduce environmental degradation. The study considered a three-echelon SCM for tires by evaluating the effectiveness of smart energy use in production, lighting, air handling, and management for reducing emissions, enhancing profitability, and reducing dependency on NRNE sources. The study is modified to incorporate a non-linear demand function for the selling price and a variable production system that considers remanufacturing returned EOL tires to address research gaps. It established a relation between carbon emissions and remanufacturing strategy. The following Section contains a brief definition of the problem, the parameters used to formulate the model, and the hypotheses to validate the model.

Problem definition, notation, and assumptions

This section briefly presents the problem formulation, the notation defined to establish the model, the essential assumptions developed to validate the model, the mathematical formulation of each player, and the solution methodology and algorithm.

Problem definition

An outline of the present study is depicted in Fig. 3. The figure illustrates that all CTSCM players use smart energy to make SCM sustainable, reducing environmental impact and enhancing profits. The CTSCM is established with the presence of a manufacturer, a third-party logistics (3PL), and multiple retailers. All players use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% smart energy, meaning they consume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% renewable energy and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\alpha )$$\end{document}% traditional energy. Renewable energy sources, like solar energy, emit zero carbon and are inexpensive. Therefore, the smart energy policies adopted by the players abate emissions and reduce energy-related costs. The manufacturer needs energy to run the hybrid production system, the building management system (BMS), lighting, and common services (CMS), whereas 3PLs and retailers consume energy for lighting and air-handling for inventory management. With the use of the smart energy policy, the manufacturer follows a flexible manufacturing-remanufacturing strategy to reduce waste in the form of EOL tires. The manufacturer sends new and remanufactured tires to all retailers according to their demand. The manufacturer follows an ordering schedule determined by the retailer’s lead time and demand. On the other hand, 3PL collects EOL tires from end customers by paying a per-unit fee. The collection volume may not be the same every time. Therefore, the collection rate of EOL tires is considered a variable. The 3PL provides all transportation for the forward and reverse SC, as well as containers, to the manufacturer for shipping tires to retailers. The 3PL is divided into two segments to address limitations in capacity. One (3PL-I) collects and recycles EOL tires; the second (3PL-II) maintains containers and stores recycled tires received from 3PL-I and sent to the manufacturing plant. Finally, all retailers sell tires in the market at a flexible price. This model analyzes price-dependent quadratic demand. The transportation sector is one of the most liable sectors for emissions. The SC is closed, and the volume of transportation is high; therefore, emissions are high, too. The model takes into account both fixed and variable transportation and related emissions. The carbon tax strategy is applied to reduce transportation emissions. The study is developed for bus and truck tires. The flow of products is demonstrated in Fig. 3.Fig. 3System description of the CLSCM including 3PL.

System description of the CLSCM including 3PL.

Description of decision variables and parameters used to formulate the model.

Assumptions

It is required to develop some assumptions for the simplification of the proposed model. Those assumptions are as follows: A three-echelon CLSCM for tires is found with a manufacturer, a 3PL, and multiple retailers. In an SCM, electricity is a basic need for distinct players with different purposes. In particular, the study finds that the manufacturer needs energy for the production of final products, building management services, common services, and lighting purposes. In the case of 3PLs and retailers, the study finds the energy used for air handling and lighting56.The study found that smart energy systems provide a combination of renewable and conventional energy, which is both economically and environmentally beneficial. The study established that all players involved in the SCM consume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% of smart energy, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% renewable energy and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\alpha )$$\end{document}% traditional energy. Emissions from renewable energy are negligible and are considered zero for the study.The manufacturer produces new tires under flexible production systems at a variable production rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m$$\end{document} units per unit of time. The price to make a unit product is found to be a function of the production rate, which depends on the development and tool/die costs required to run a successful production system. The price for making a unit product is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( b_0+\frac{b_1}{\rho _m}+b_2\rho _m\right)$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0$$\end{document} is per unit raw material cost, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_1$$\end{document} is development cost, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_2$$\end{document} is tool/die cost57. Moreover, the manufacturer remanufactures collected EOL tires at a fixed per-unit price, and the remanufacturing rate is assumed to equal the collection rate of EOL tires58.In reality, more than one retailer sells the same products at different prices across different geographic regions. In the literature, researchers found that demand changes inversely with price changes, i.e., with a price increase, demand for the product decreases, and vice versa59,60. The study established a price-dependent demand, with demand decreasing quadratically with the product’s price. Timely delivery of products is a key to success in any business. The manufacturer delivered tires to multi-retailers according to a delivery schedule that depends on tire demand and retailers’ lead times. The manufacturer sells both new and remanufactured at the same price to all retailers61.

In a CLSCM, the 3PL plays a significant role. The proposed study split the 3PL into two parts for the capacity issues. 3PL-I collects EOL tires from consumers, offers customers incentives, and recycles them. The recycled EOL tires are then sent to the second component of 3PL (3PL-II). When the manufacturer orders EOL tires, 3PL-II provides those to the manufacturer. Furthermore, 3PL-II provides containers and transportation to manufacturers to distribute tires to retailers.

The proposed study split the 3PL into two parts for the capacity issues. 3PL-I collects EOL tires from consumers, offers customers incentives, and recycles them. The recycled EOL tires are then sent to the second component of 3PL (3PL-II). When the manufacturer orders EOL tires, 3PL-II provides those to the manufacturer. Furthermore, 3PL-II provides containers and transportation to manufacturers to distribute tires to retailers.

The logistics system in an SCM plays a vital role in timely delivery. Again, consuming EOL tires in a closed-loop CTSCM is essential to reduce waste tires by remanufacturing. Transportation and emissions are related to each other. Therefore, a fixed and a variable carbon-emissions cost is imposed on transportation62,63.

Mathematical expression of the model

This study develops a sustainable CLSCM for tires. The CLSCM is established in the context of multiple retailers, a manufacturer, and a third-party (3PL). The manufacturer produces new tires under a flexible manufacturing system, remanufactures EOL tires collected, and delivers the final products to all retailers in accordance with a delivery schedule aligned with retailers’ lead times and demand. All retailers sell final products to end consumers at variable prices. The 3PL provides containers to the manufacturer for delivering final products to retailers. The 3PL collects and supplies EOL products to the manufacturer for remanufacturing. The energy consumption costs for lighting and air handling are considered for all three players of the CLSCM. Further, the manufacturer considers the energy consumption for production, building management systems, and common services. Not all manufactured and remanufactured tires are flawless; therefore, the manufacturer incurs a predetermined cost to enhance quality. The manufacturer incurs a loss of goodwill due to the tire’s inferior quality, and this cost is accounted for in this study. The different costs of the three parties are described in the following:

Retailer’s cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TC_r$$\end{document})

The study finds that there are n retailers from different regions that sell final products to end consumers at flexible prices. Each retailer orders new tires from the manufacturer to satisfy customer demand every cycle with a flexible selling price. The basic costs of retailers are considered in formulating the model: ordering costs, holding costs, and energy consumption costs. The elementary description of these costs is given below.

Revenue (RR)

At a rate $\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_i}$$\end{document}, final items are sold to customers by the retailer i. Then, the revenue from retailer i is obtained by multiplying the per-unit price by the retailer i’s demand. Therefore, the revenue of the retailer i is1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_{p_i}r_{ri} ~~~~i = 1, 2, . . ., n. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence, total revenue of n retailers is obtained by summation for n retailers.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} RR = \sum _{i=1}^{n}S_{p_i}r_{ri} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Ordering cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{or}$$\end{document})

The cycle length of each retailer in the proposed model is the same. Each retailer spends a fixed amount, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_r$$\end{document}, to order final products from the manufacturer. The ordering costs generally include email, phone, and brokerage fees, as well as taxes and surcharges associated with the ordering process. Therefore, the total ordering cost per cycle for all retailers is calculated by dividing the ordering cost by the cycle length (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}) and summing over n retailers. Thus, ordering costs for all retailers is3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{or} =\sum _{i=1}^{n} \frac{G_{ri}}{\tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Holding cost

The retailer keeps and maintains the final products after receiving them from the manufacturer, so the retailer needs some space until all products are sold. In this process, the retailer has to pay a certain amount. In the proposed study, the inventory characteristic for retailers follows the elementary economic order quantity model. The retailer receives the delivery of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau r_{ri}$$\end{document}. The average inventory for the ith retailer over time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\tau r_{ri}}{2}$$\end{document}. Therefore, the storage expenses of the seller i is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_r\frac{\tau r_{ri}}{2}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_r$$\end{document} is per unit holding cost. Hence, the total holding cost for n retailers is calculated by summing over n retailers.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{hr} = \sum _{i=1}^{n}H_r\frac{\tau r_{ri}}{2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy consumption cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ECC_R$$\end{document})

Retailers use the building for two primary purposes: conducting official work and storing tires for sale64. The retailer requires energy for lighting and air handling for inventory management and official works. Apart from traditional energy, retailers use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% smart energy of the total energy they use. The total energy used by the retailer is calculated as follows:

Air handling cost

The retailer i uses a fan for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1^i$$\end{document} hours for air handling per day. It is assumed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^r_{j(ca)}$$\end{document} is the horsepower energy consumed per hour/fan. At first, the horsepower is converted into watts by multiplying by 746. After that, the total energy consumed in a month is calculated by multiplying the energy consumed in an hour by a single fan by the number of fans used and 30 (days in a month). Then, the total consumed energy is converted to kilowatts by dividing it by 1000. Therefore, the total consumed energy per cycle for air handling for retailer i is calculated as follows:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{f_i\times P^r_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _1^i \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Lighting cost

The retailer i uses the lights for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _2^i$$\end{document} hours per day to clean the floor, maintain inventory, and conduct official work. The retailer uses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^r_{j(ca)}$$\end{document} bulbs for management. The total energy consumed by all bulbs is obtained by multiplying the energy consumption (watts) of a single bulb by the number of bulbs used. Then multiply it by 30 to get the monthly energy consumption for lighting. Finally, divide it by 1000 to convert total consumption into kilowatts. Therefore, the energy consumption for lighting per cycle of the retailer i is calculated as below:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{B^r_{j(ca)}\times P^r_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _2^i \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Hence, total energy consumption for n retailers per cycle is calculated as follows.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TE_R = \sum _{i=1}^{n}\bigg (\frac{f_i\times P^r_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _1^i \times 30}{1000\times \tau }+\frac{B^r_{j(ca)}\times P^r_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _2^i \times 30}{1000\times \tau }\bigg ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The retailer i uses smart electricity, which combines renewable and non-renewable energy. That is the retailer pays for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% renewable energy and rest \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\alpha )$$\end{document}% non-renewable energy at a rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ec_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ec_2$$\end{document} ($/unit), respectively. Then, the total energy cost for all retailers is8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ECC_R=\left( ec_1\times \alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\right) TE_R. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Now, the total cost for n retailer is9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TC_r = \sum _{i=1}^{n} \frac{G_{ri}}{\tau } + \sum _{i=1}^{n}H_r\frac{\tau r_{ri}}{2}+ECC_R. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Model for the manufacturer

The producer produces new products under a flexible production system and remanufactures EOL products collected. Then, the manufacturer delivers the final products to all retailers with a specified delivery schedule. The manufacturer ordered used tires with the least quality, Q, from 3PL. The basic costs to the producer are setup, ordering, storage costs for new and EOL products, energy consumption costs, manufacturing and remanufacturing costs, goodwill loss costs, and quality degradation costs.

Setup cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{ms}$$\end{document})

The setup is a crucial step in the production process for any manufacturing industry. Before the start of production, every manufacturing industry goes through machinery setup, inspection, and other tasks to ensure a smooth, successful production process. Therefore, at the beginning of each production period, the manufacturer must incur costs to establish a proper manufacturing setup. In this proposed study, the setup expenses per cycle for the producer are calculated.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{ms} = \frac{B_m}{\tau } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Ordering cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{om}$$\end{document})

The remanufacturing of EOL tires is both economically and environmentally beneficial. For remanufacturing EOL tires, the manufacturer must collect them from the end market or a third party. In the present model, the manufacturer is found to collect EOL tires from a third party. For this, the manufacturer must place an order with the 3PL for EOL tires as needed. If the manufacturer pays an amount of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_m$$\end{document} $ in a single order, then the ordering cost of the manufacturer per cycle is obtained by dividing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_m$$\end{document} by the cycle length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}. Therefore, the producer’s ordering expenses per cycle are calculated as follows.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{om} = \frac{G_m}{\tau } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Holding cost for new tire (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{hns}$$\end{document})

The manufacturer starts the production process based on the total order amount received from all retailers. The manufacturer delivers the ordered quantity to all retailers upon completion of the production process. Therefore, the manufacturer must store the final products in their warehouse throughout the production process. After that, the manufacturer delivers the ordered quantity to all retailers according to a predefined delivery schedule that depends on the retailer’s ordered quantity and the delivery lead time. Now, the total inventory for the final products of the producer is derived as follows:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&= \frac{1}{2}\frac{q^2}{\rho _m} + \frac{q^2}{d} - L_1P_1 - L_2(P_1 + P_2) - L_3(P_1 + P_2 + P_3)- . . . - (L_n)(P_1 + P_2 + P_3 + . . . + P_n)\nonumber \\&= \frac{(d\tau )^2}{2\rho _m} + \frac{(d\tau )^2}{d} - \tau [L_1r_{r1} + L_2(r_{r1} + r_{r2}) + L_3(r_{r1} + r_{r2} + r_{r3}) + . . . + (L_n)(r_{r1} + r_{r2} + r_{r3} + . . . + r_{rn})]\nonumber \\&= \frac{d\tau ^2(d + 2 \rho _m)}{2\rho _m} - \tau \bigg [\sum _{i=1}^{n}L_i\sum _{j=1}^{i}r_{rj}\bigg ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In that case, the average inventory in each period is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}& \frac{d\tau (d + 2 \rho _m)}{2\rho _m} - \bigg [\sum _{i=1}^{n}L_i\sum _{j=1}^{i}r_{rj}\bigg ].&\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Thus, the holding cost for the new tires of the manufacturer per unit time is13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{hns} = H_m\bigg [\frac{d\tau (d + 2 \rho _m)}{2\rho _m} - \sum _{i=1}^{n}L_i\sum _{j=1}^{i}r_{rj}\bigg ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fig. 4Finished product inventory.

Finished product inventory.

Holding cost for used product (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{hc}$$\end{document})

The manufacturer places an order for EOL tires with the 3PL after completing the production process and stores those EOL products until the start of another production cycle. From Fig. 4, it is clear that holding of the EOL tires is the aggregate of the rectangle under setup time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau - q/\rho _m$$\end{document} and area of the triangle over production runtime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(q/\rho _m)$$\end{document}. The total inventory of EOL tires is calculated as follows:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&= \beta d(\tau - q/\rho _m) + \frac{\beta d q}{2\rho _m}= \beta d\bigg (\tau - \sum _{i=1}^{n}r_{ri}\tau /\rho _m\bigg ) + \frac{\beta d\sum _{i=1}^{n}r_{ri}\tau }{2\rho _m} = \beta d\bigg (\tau - \frac{\sum _{i=1}^{n}r_{ri} \tau }{2\rho _m}\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Therefore, the average inventory for the EOL tires is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta d\bigg (1 - \frac{\sum _{i=1}^n r_{ri}}{2\rho _m}\bigg )$$\end{document}.

Now, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m'$$\end{document} is the per unit holding cost of EOL tires. Hence the storage cost of the producer for EOL tires is15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{hc} = H_m'\beta d\bigg (1 - \frac{\sum _{i=1}^n r_{ri}}{2\rho _m}\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Manufacturing cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{MC}$$\end{document})

The manufacturer produces new tires from raw materials under a flexible production system. A flexible production system is very useful for controlling production rates in response to product demand. The per unit production cost is a function of the production rate, which is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( b_0+\frac{b_1}{\rho _m}+b_2\rho _m\right)$$\end{document} 65. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0$$\end{document} is the per unit raw material cost ($/unit), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_1$$\end{document} is the development costs, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_2$$\end{document} is the tool/die costs associated with the flexible production system. The manufacturer produces new tires and remanufactures EOL tires. The manufacturer produces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d(1 - \beta )$$\end{document} units of new products at a variable production rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m$$\end{document} units/unit time. Hence, the total manufacturing costs are obtained as follows:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{MC} = \left( b_0+\frac{b_1}{\rho _m}+b_2\rho _m\right) d(1 - \beta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Remanufacturing cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{RC}$$\end{document})

In response to governmental pressure and heightened consumer environmental consciousness, industries are investing in the production of eco-friendly products by minimizing resource consumption and mitigating risks through the reuse, remanufacturing, and recycling of EOL products. The remanufacturing of EOL tires is economically advantageous and environmentally sustainable. Consequently, the manufacturer purchases EOL tires from the 3PL at a rate of $\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_3$$\end{document}/unit. The manufacturer allocates $\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_r$$\end{document}/unit to remanufacture each end-of-life tire to restore it to a condition equivalent to that of a new product. Consequently, the manufacturer’s remanufacturing expense is17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{RC} = \beta d(c_r + c_3). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy consumption cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ECC_M$$\end{document})

Energy for machine

Energy is a primary tool for any production system. The production process is interrupted for various reasons, and improper energy supply is one of them. An interruption in the production process results in lower profits for the manufacturer. Therefore, the manufacturer drives the machine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _3$$\end{document} hours per day to continue the production process. Energy consumed for a machine per hour is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^m_{j(hp)}$$\end{document} horsepower. The energy required per cycle is calculated by multiplying the number of hours the machine runs by 30 days, then dividing by the cycle time. Total energy consumption for the machine run per cycle is as follows:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{M^m_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _3 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy for BMS

One of the most essential services in a manufacturing plant is building management service(BMS). This service includes Maintenance, testing, inspections, cleaning, fire safety, and operations. If an average of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{MS}$$\end{document} kWh of energy is used for BMS, then the total energy used for BMS per cycle of the manufacturer is calculated as follows:19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{B_{MS}}{\tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy for common services

Common services and utilities in a tire manufacturing plant include cooling water, chiller, heat pump, compressor, boiler, and vacuum56. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{CS}$$\end{document} is the required monthly energy for common service, then the total energy used for common services by the manufacturing plant per cycle is calculated as follows:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{E_{CS}}{\tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy for lighting

Proper lighting is one of the most critical components of any production system. Light is essential to a production system for running the production process and managing inventory. LED high bay lights are used in the tire manufacturing plant for lighting. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{j=1}^{\Omega }F^m_{j(ca)}$$\end{document} be the number of high bay lights used for lighting. The total energy consumed by all bulbs is obtained by multiplying the energy consumption (watts) of a single bulb by the number of bulbs used. Then multiply it by 30 to get the monthly energy consumption for lighting. Finally, divide it by 1000 to convert total consumption into kilowatts. Therefore, the energy consumption for lighting per cycle of the manufacturer is calculated as follows:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{F^m_{j(ca)}\times P^m_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _5 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Therefore, the overall expenses for consuming energy by the producer per cycle is22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TE_M = \frac{M^m_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _3 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }+\frac{B_{MS}}{\tau }+\frac{E_{CS}}{\tau }+\frac{F^m_{j(ca)}\times P^m_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _5 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The manufacturer uses smart electricity, which combines renewable and non-renewable energy. That is the retailer pays for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% renewable energy and rest \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1-\alpha )$$\end{document}% non-renewable energy at a rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ec_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ec_2$$\end{document} ($/unit), respectively. Then, the total energy cost of the manufacturer is23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&ECC_M =\left( ec_1\times \alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\right) TE_M\nonumber \\&=\left( ec_1\times \alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\right) \bigg (\frac{M^m_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _3 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }+\frac{B_{MS} }{\tau }+\frac{E_{CS}}{\tau }+\frac{F^m_{j(ca)}\times P^m_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _5}{1000\times \tau }\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Quality upgradation cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{QM}$$\end{document})

It is considered that the average quality of the EOL tires is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_r$$\end{document}. Now, the manufacturer upgrades the quality of these EOL tires to Q during remanufacturing. To improve this quality to Q, the manufacturer must incur some cost. In this study, a quadratic function is used to model the increasing quality rate with increasing investment amount58. The manufacturer invests \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_Q$$\end{document} ($/product) to improve product quality. Therefore, the expenses of the producer to improve the quality of the product are24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{QM} = c_QQ^2d(1-\beta ) + \beta dc_Q(Q^2 - Q_r^2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Goodwill lost cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{GL}$$\end{document})

Goodwill is an intangible asset that adds value to an enterprise over time. It refers to the enterprise’s value generated by its brand, customer base, and other factors. Now, faulty, low-quality products will decrease the manufacturer’s brand value. It is considered that the manufactured and remanufactured tires are not 100% pure. Therefore, it will harm the enterprise’s brand image. Due to a goodwill loss, there will be an effect on product sales, and this loss is called the goodwill loss cost. The goodwill lost cost of the manufacturer is calculated as follows:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{GL} = (1-Q)G. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Total cost for manufacturer is26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TC_m = \frac{B_m}{\tau }+\frac{G_m}{\tau }+H_m\bigg [\frac{d\tau (d + 2 \rho _m)}{2\rho _m} - \sum _{i=1}^{n}L_i\sum _{j=1}^{i}d_j\bigg ]+(1-Q)G+H_m'\beta d\bigg (1 - \frac{\sum _{i=1}^n r_{ri}}{2\rho _m}\bigg )+c_QQ^2d(1-\beta ) \nonumber \\&+ \beta dc_Q(Q^2 - Q_r^2)+\left( b_0+\frac{b_1}{\rho _m}+b_2\rho _m\right) d(1 - \beta )+\beta d(c_r+c_3)+\left( ec_1\times \alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\right) +ECC_M. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

3PL cost

In an SCM, the 3PL plays an essential role in closing the loop. Two 3PLs are considered for the system’s incapabilities in the proposed CLSCM. The inclusions of two 3PL reflects an operational necessity in real closed-loop tire supply chains and provides several modelling advantages. It is described as follows:

Capacity and functional specialization

In the proposed study, two 3PLs use different logistics processes for the management of EOL tires. 3PL-I: The 3PL-I collects EOL tires from customers, inspects those products, and is involved in preliminary processing. 3PL-II: Whereas the 3PL-II stores EOL products after collecting them from 3PL-I and delivers them to the manufacturer as per the manufacturer’s requirements. In addition, 3PL-II provides containers to the manufacturer for the forward distribution of new tires. These activities need different capacities, infrastructure, and cost structures. For example, collection centres, named 3PL-I, are typically highly decentralized and have limited capacity; in contrast, storage/container hubs are centralized and have high capacity. Therefore, representing these two 3PLs as a single node in the proposed SCM may mask these structural constraints and misrepresent the real-world scenario.

Accurate representation of forward versus reverse flow

Further in the model, the closed-loop of the SCM is described as: The forward flow (manufacturer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} retailer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} return of empty container to 3PL-II) and the reverse flow ( collection of EOL tire by 3PL-I \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} send to 3PL-II after recycling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} shipment of EOL tires to manufacturer). However, treating two 3PLs as nodes to handle the forward and reverse flows allows the model to capture crucial entities separately. These entities are: holding cost dynamics at two nodes, the pattern of smart energy use, separate transportation costs, and emissions contributions. Therefore, splitting 3PL into two nodes realistically represents real-world scenarios and, rather than increasing complexity, enhances the model’s analytical usefulness. The elementary costs related to 3PLs are discussed in the following:

3PL-I cost

The servicing centre is considered 3Pl-I. It collects used tires from customers by paying an incentive of the amount \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1 \$/\text{unit }$$\end{document}. The collection period of the 3PL-I is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\tau }{y_1}$$\end{document}, and at the end of each period, it provides the EOL tires to the 3PL-II on its own expense. The costs related to it are described below.

Setup cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SC_{3PL}$$\end{document})

A setup is essential to start any business. The tasks of any third-party logistics are to collect EOL products from end users and send them to a player in an SCM. Therefore, for EOL tire collection, handling, storage, and recycling, the 3PL-I needs a basic setup. The 3PL-I completes the setup process at the beginning of each cycle, which requires him to spend some money. Therefore, the setup cost per cycle for the 3PL-I is calculated as follows.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} SC_{3PL} = \frac{B_{31}y_1}{\tau } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Collection cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CC_{3PLI}$$\end{document})

The 3PL-I gathers EOL tires from consumers at a rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}. Then, the total units of EOL tires collected is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\beta$$\end{document}. At first, 3PL-I invests a certain amount in the collection centre and arranges the inspection system. The 3PL-I pays \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1(\$/unit)$$\end{document} to the customer. The total collection cost is28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} CC_{3PLI} = a\beta ^2 + d\beta c_1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Holding cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$HC_{3PLI}$$\end{document})

The 3PL-I collects and inspects EOL tires, then stores them in the storeroom until they are dispatched to the 3PL-II. In each cycle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau /y_1$$\end{document}, the 3PL-I collects an equal volume of EOL tires, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d\beta }{y_1}$$\end{document} units, and sells them at the end of that cycle. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{31}$$\end{document} is the per-unit storage expense of an EOL tire, the total holding cost per cycle is29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} HC_{3PLI} = h_{31}\frac{d\beta \tau }{2y_1^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy consumption cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ECC_{3PLI}$$\end{document})

In the inventory management process, the 3PL-I has to incur costs for energy consumption for lighting and air handling. The 3PL-I uses smart energy rather than only traditional energy to reduce energy consumption costs. The total energy required for 3PL-I is calculated as follows:

Energy for air handling

For official works, such as storing EOL tires and inspecting those, the 3PL-I uses fans for air handling.
