The 3PL-I uses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_2$$\end{document} fans for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _6$$\end{document} daily hours. At first, the horsepower is converted into watts by multiplying by 746. After that, the total energy consumed in a month is calculated by multiplying the energy consumed in an hour by a single fan by the number of fans used and 30 (days in a month). Then, the total consumed energy is converted to kilowatts by dividing it by 1000. Therefore, the total consumed energy per cycle for air handling for the 3PL-I is calculated as follows:30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{F_2\times P^{31}_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _6 \times 30}{1000\times \frac{\tau }{y_1}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy for lightning

Light energy is required for official work, such as storing and recycling EOL tires for the 3PL-I. The 3PL-I uses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^{31}_{j(ca)}$$\end{document} number of bulbs every day for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _8$$\end{document} hours. The total energy consumed by all bulbs is obtained by multiplying the energy consumption (watts) of a single bulb by the number of bulbs used. Then multiply it by 30 to get the monthly energy consumption for lighting. Finally, divide it by 1000 to convert total consumption into kilowatts. Therefore, the energy consumption for lighting per cycle of the 3PL-I is calculated as follows:31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{B^{31}_{j(ca)}\times P^{31}_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _8 \times 30}{1000\times \frac{\tau }{y_1}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The total energy cost of the 3PL-I is coming form air handling and lighting. Therefore, total energy used by 3PL-I is given as below:32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TE_{3PLI} = \bigg (\frac{F_2\times P^{31}_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _6 \times 30}{1000\times \frac{\tau }{y_1}}+\frac{B^{31}_{j(ca)}\times P^{31}_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _8 \times 30}{1000\times \frac{\tau }{y_1}}\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Similar to others player 3PL-I use smart energy of which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% is renewable energy and rest is non-renewable energy. The overall energy expenses by the 3PL-I is33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&ECC_{3PLI}= (ec_1\alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha ))\times TE_{3PLI} =(ec_1\alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha ))\times \bigg (\frac{F_2\times P^{31}_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _6 \times 30}{1000\times \frac{\tau }{y_1}}+\frac{B^{31}_{j(ca)}\times P^{31}_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _8 \times 30}{1000\times \frac{\tau }{y_1}}\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Transportation cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TCC_{3PLI}$$\end{document})

3PL-I collects EOL tires from customers and holds them at its store until they are sent to 3PL-II. At the end of the cycle, the 3PL-I delivers EOL tires to the 3PL-II. It is considered that the transportation cost for EOL tires is borne by 3PL-I. It is taken that 3PL-I follows a multiple-delivery policy. Therefore, per cycle, the fixed and variable transportation costs are34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TCC_{3PLI} = \frac{(F_T + V_Td\beta )y_1}{\tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The total cost of the 3PL-I is35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TC_{3PLI} = \frac{B_{31}y_1}{\tau }+ a\beta ^2 + d\beta c_1 + h_{31}\frac{d\beta \tau }{2y_1^2}+ \frac{(F_T + V_Td\beta )y_1}{\tau }+(ec_1\alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha ))+ECC_{3PLI}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

3PL-II cost

The main task of 3PL-II is to provide containers to meet the manufacturer’s demand for supplying new tires to all retailers, and the other task is to collect EOL tires from 3PL-II to meet the manufacturer’s demand for remanufacturing. The costs of the 3PL-II are as follows.

Setup cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SC_{3PLII}$$\end{document})

A setup is fundamental to starting any business. The 3PL-II collects EOL tires from 3PL-I and stores them, along with containers, for delivering new tires from the manufacturer to all retailers. Therefore, for the collection, transportation, storage, and handling of tires and containers, the 3PL-II requires a basic setup. The 3PL-II completes the setup process at the beginning of each cycle, which costs him some money. Therefore, the 3PL-I setup cost per cycle is calculated as follows.36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} SC_{3PLII} = \frac{B_{32}}{\tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Holding cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$HC_{3PLII}$$\end{document})

The 3PL-II collects used tires from the 3PL-I by paying a specific per-unit cost for EOL tires. These used tires are sold to the manufacturer for remanufacturing. The time duration between receiving used tires from 3PL-I and delivering to the manufacturer, 3PL-II has to carry these EOL tires in the store. It is assumed that the 3PL-II must spend \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{32}$$\end{document} $/unit for storage. The storage cost of 3PL-II is determined by multiplying the unit cost \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{32}$$\end{document} with the average stock of EOL tires. Hence, the storage expenses per period for 3PL-II are37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} HC_{3PLII} = h_{32}\frac{d\beta \tau }{2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy consumption cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ECC_{3PLII}$$\end{document})

To hold the EOL tires and containers, the 3PL-II has to spend some costs for energy consumption due to lightning and air handling. These cost are calculated as follows.

Energy for air handling

For official works, storing EOL tires and containers, the 3PL-II uses fans for air handling. The 3PL-II uses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_3$$\end{document} fans for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _7$$\end{document} hours daily. At first, the horsepower is converted into watts by multiplying by 746. After that, the total energy consumed in a month is calculated by multiplying the energy consumed in an hour by a single fan by the number of fans used and 30 (days in a month). Then, the total consumed energy is converted to kilowatts by dividing it by 1000. Therefore, the total consumed energy per cycle for air handling for the 3PL-II is calculated as follows:38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{F_3\times P^{32}_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _7 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Energy for lightning

Energy for light is required for official work, such as storing and recycling EOL tires for the 3PL-II. The 3PL-II uses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^{32}_{j(ca)}$$\end{document} number of bulbs every day for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _9$$\end{document} hours. The total energy consumed by all bulbs is obtained by multiplying the energy consumption (watts) of a single bulb by the number of bulbs used. Then multiply it by 30 to get the monthly energy consumption for lighting. Finally, divide it by 1000 to convert total consumption into kilowatts. Therefore, the energy consumption for lighting per cycle of the 3PL-I is calculated as follows:39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{B^{32}_{j(ca)}\times P^{32}_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _9 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Similar to others player, 3PL-II use smart energy of which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}% is renewable energy and rest is non-renewable energy. Then the total cost that has to pay for using energy by the 3PL-I is40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TE_{3PLII} = \frac{F_{32}\times P^{32}_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _7 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }+ \frac{B^{32}_{j(ca)}\times P^{32}_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _9 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Therefore, the total cost paid by 3PL-II for using energy is41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&ECC_{3PLII}=(ec_1\alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha ))\times TE_{3PLII}= (ec_1\alpha + ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\times \bigg (\frac{F_3\times P^{32}_{j(hp)}\times 746_{\frac{w}{hp}}\times \zeta _7 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }+ \frac{B^{32}_{j(ca)}\times P^{32}_{j(\frac{w}{bulb})}\times \zeta _9 \times 30}{1000\times \tau }\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Transportation cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TCC_{3PLII}$$\end{document})

The 3PL-II provides containers to transport new tires from the manufacturer to all retailers, and empty containers are returned to 3PL-II upon delivery completion. At the end of each period \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}, the 3PL-II supplies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\beta$$\end{document} units of EOL tires to the manufacturer. The overall shipping expenses of the 3PL-II are the aggregate of the following three:

(i) The shipping cost to supply new tires from the manufacturer to all retailers is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t\big (\delta _{im}(\frac{P_i}{\sigma })\big ).$$\end{document}

(ii) the transportation cost for returning empty container is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t(\delta _{ik_1}(\frac{P_i}{\sigma })).$$\end{document}

(iii) the transportation cost for providing EOL tires to the manufacturer is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t(\delta _{k_1m}(\frac{d\beta }{\sigma }))$$\end{document}.

Hence the overall shipping charge of the 3PL-II is42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TCC_{3PLII} = c_t\bigg [\sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}(\frac{P_i}{\sigma }) + \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}(\frac{P_i}{\sigma }) + \delta _{km}(\frac{d\beta }{\sigma })\bigg ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Container management cost (CMC)

It is vital to determine the number of containers to ensure the smooth flow of goods between the node (producer, retailer) and from 3PL-II to the manufacturer, without increasing the system’s cost too much. Therefore, to optimize the storage expenses for containers in the system, the maximum number of containers used is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lceil \frac{d_{max}\tau }{\sigma }\rceil$$\end{document} 66. Therefore, the container management cost is43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} CMC = p\sigma ^{t-1}d_{max}\tau. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}t provides the reference between the management cost of the container and its capacity. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>1$$\end{document}, the management cost of a small container is less than the large container, and when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t<1$$\end{document}, the management cost for a small container is much more than a large container.

Cost for holding the container (CHC)

The 3PL-II provides containers for transporting finished tires to all retailers. Therefore, 3PL-II must store the containers in its storeroom. For a smooth flow of product, the maximum number of containers used is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lceil \frac{q_{max}}{\sigma }\rceil$$\end{document}, which is required for the delivery of the largest single delivery. After that, the deliveries are small, and the extra container has to be held by the 3PL-II. The average inventory is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigg (\sum _{i=1}^{n} L_i\bigg (\frac{d_{max}-r_{ri}}{\sigma }\bigg ) + (\tau - \sum _{i=1}^nL_i)\frac{d_{max}}{\sigma }\bigg )$$\end{document}. Therefore, the inventory holding cost is44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} CHC = h_c\bigg (\sum _{i=1}^{n} L_i\bigg (\frac{d_{max}-r_{ri}}{\sigma }\bigg ) + (\tau - \sum _{i=1}^nL_i)\frac{d_{max}}{\sigma }\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The total cost 3PL-II is summarized in the following equation as follows:45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TC_{3PL-II} = \frac{B_{32}}{\tau }+h_{32}\frac{d\beta \tau }{2}+p\sigma ^{t-1}d_{max}\tau + h_c\bigg (\sum _{i=1}^{n} L_i\bigg (\frac{d_{max}-r_{ri}}{\sigma }\bigg ) + (\tau - \sum _{i=1}^nL_i)\frac{d_{max}}{\sigma }\bigg )+ECC_{3PL-II}+c_t\bigg [\sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}(\frac{r_{ri}\tau }{\sigma }) + \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}(\frac{r_{ri}\tau }{\sigma }) + \delta _{km}(\frac{d\beta }{\sigma })\bigg ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Cost regarding carbon emissions

Carbon is emitted into the environment due to transportation in both forward SCM and reverse SCM. Two types of carbon emissions costs are considered: a fixed cost related to distance, fuel, etc., and a variable cost depending on shipment size.

Emissions in forward SC (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CEF_S$$\end{document})

3PL sends the container to the manufacturer in the forward supply chain. The producer sends the loaded containers to retailers. Throughout the entire transportation process, carbon is emitted. The total emissions cost in the forward supply chain management is given as follows:46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} CEF_S = \sum _{i=1}^n D_{fi} + \sum _{i=1}^nD_vr_{ri}\tau. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Emissions in reverse SC (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CEB_s$$\end{document})

In the reverse supply chain, the 3PL collects the EOL tires from customers. Then, the 3PL sends those tires to the producer as needed. The total process includes transportation, during which some carbon is emitted. The total emissions cost in the reverse supply chain is given below:47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} CEB_s = \sum _{i=1}^n D_{fri} + D_{vr}\beta d. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Emissions calculation for using traditional energy

Traditional energy produced by burning fossil fuels, which is why a huge amount of CO2 is emitted into the environment. For producing 1 million BTU energy an average of K1 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {pounds}^2$$\end{document} (https://www.eia.gov/tools/faqs/faq.php?id=74&t=11, accessed on 19.04.2023 at 07:56 am IST) CO2 emitted. Again 1 million BTU energy equivalent to K2 kwh \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {energy}^3$$\end{document} (https://www.inchcalculator.com/convert/kilowatt-hour-to-million-btu/), which means that to produce 1 kwh energy approximately \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_E$$\end{document} kg CO2 emitted. Thus, the total CO2 emissions in this proposed supply chain for using traditional energy is48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} TCE=C_E\times (1-\alpha )\times \bigg (TE_R + TE_M + TE_{31} + TE_{32}\bigg ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Profit calculation

Total profit of the SCM is calculated by subtracting total cost of the manufacturer, retailers, 3PLs cost, and emissions cost from the revenue.

Therefore total profit = Revenue - (Manufacturer’s cost + Retailer cost + 3PL cost + Carbon emissions cost)49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TP(\beta , S_{p_i}, \tau , \sigma , \rho _m)=\sum _{i=1}^{n}S_{p_i}r_{ri} - \bigg [\sum _{i=1}^{n} \frac{G_{ri}}{\tau }+ \sum _{i=1}^{n}H_r\frac{\tau r_{ri}}{2} + \left( ec_1\times \alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\right) TE_R + \frac{B_m}{\tau } + \frac{G_m}{\tau } \nonumber \\&+H_m\bigg [\frac{d\tau (d + 2 \rho _m)}{2\rho _m} - \sum _{i=1}^{n}L_i\sum _{j=1}^{i}r_{rj}\bigg ]+ H_m'\beta d\bigg (1 - \frac{\sum _{i=1}^n r_{ri}}{2\rho _m}\bigg ) + \left( b_0+\frac{b_1}{\rho _m}+b_2\rho _m\right) d(1 - \beta )+ \beta d(c_r + c_3)\nonumber \\&+ \left( ec_1\times \alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha )\right) TE_M + c_QQ^2d(1-\beta ) + \beta dc_Q(Q^2 - Q_r^2) + (1-Q)G + \frac{B_{31}y_1}{\tau } + a\beta ^2 + d\beta c_1 \nonumber \\&+ h_{31}\frac{d\beta \tau }{2y_1^2} + (ec_1\alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha ))\times TE_{3PLI} + \frac{(F_T + V_Td\beta )y_1}{\tau } + \frac{B_{32}}{\tau } + h_{32}\frac{d\beta \tau }{2}\nonumber \\&+ (ec_1\alpha +ec_2\times (1-\alpha ))\times TE_{3PLII} + c_t\bigg [\sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}(\frac{P_i}{\sigma }) + \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}(\frac{P_i}{\sigma }) + \delta _{km}(\frac{d\beta }{\sigma })\bigg ] + p\sigma ^{t-1}d_{max}\tau \nonumber \\&+ h_c\bigg (\sum _{i=1}^{n} L_i\bigg (\frac{d_{max}-r_{ri}}{\sigma }\bigg ) + (\tau - \sum _{i=1}^nL_i)\frac{d_{max}}{\sigma }\bigg ) + \sum _{i=1}^n D_{fi} + \sum _{i=1}^nD_vr_{ri}\tau + \sum _{i=1}^n D_{fri} + D_{vr}\beta d\bigg ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TE_R$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TE_m$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TE_{3PLI}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TE_{3PLII}$$\end{document} represent total energy consumption per cycle by retailers, manufacturer, 3PL-I, and 3PL-II, respectively. Furthermore, these terms are defined in Equations 7, 22, 32, and 40, respectively.

Solution methodology

The proposed model identifies a three-echelon sustainable CLSCM comprising a manufacturer, a third party, and multiple retailers. The study maximizes total profit by leveraging smart energy and a price-dependent quadratic demand, along with the manufacturer’s hybrid production strategy. However, the formulated profit function given by the Eq. 49 is a mixed integer nonlinear function (MINLP). The non-integer decision variables are the final product price at different retail stores, cycle time, production rate, and the return rate of the EOL product. The integer variable represents the container’s capacity. A classical optimization technique is used to find the global maximum profit and the corresponding unique values of the decision variables. The necessary and sufficient conditions of the classical optimization technique are expressed below to prove the global optimality of the profit function.

Necessary condition

According to the classical optimization technique, the unique value of the non-integer decision variables can be achieved by differentiating the profit function 49 partially with respect to decision variables (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}) and then equating it to zero. In this process, one can get the unique values of all decision variables, which are listed as follows:50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\beta ^* = \frac{1}{2a}\left( \Theta _1 - \Theta _2\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\tau ^*= \sqrt{\frac{\Theta _3}{\Theta _4}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&S_{p_i}^* = \frac{1}{2y_i}\left[ \frac{r_{r_i}}{\Gamma _i}-x_i\right] ,~~\text{ i } \text{= } \text{1, } \text{2, } \text{3, } \text{. } \text{. } \text{., } \text{ n }\end{aligned}$$\end{document}53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\rho _m^* = \sqrt{\frac{d[H_md\tau -H_m'\beta \sum _{i=1}^{n}r_{ri}+2(1-\beta )c_1]}{2c_2d(1-\beta )}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Now, the optimal value of the integer variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} (capacity of the container) can be achieved from the total profit function 49 through the use of the following inequality.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&TP(\sigma ^*-1)\le TP(\sigma ^*)\ge TP(\sigma ^*+1).&\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 1

The optimal value of the integer variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} can be obtained if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _1\ge 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _2\ge 0$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _1=pd_{max}\tau \left[ (\sigma -1)^{t-1}-\sigma ^{t-1}\right] +\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma (\sigma -1)}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _2=pd_{max}\tau \left[ (\sigma +1)^{t-1}-\sigma ^{t-1}\right] -\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma (\sigma +1)}$$\end{document}.

Proof

First, regrouping the total profit function, one can write it as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TP(\beta , S_{p_i}, \tau , \sigma , \rho _m) =\mathbb {A}-\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma }-pd_{max}\tau \sigma ^{t-1}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}$$\end{document} is the sum of revenue and all costs which does not include \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} in the total profit function (Eq. 49) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {B} = h_c \bigg( \sum _{i=1}^{n} L_i\left( d_{max}-r_{ri}\right)$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+ (\tau - \sum _{i=1}^nL_i)d_{max}\bigg) +c_t\left[ \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}(r_{ri}\tau ) + \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}(r_{ri}\tau ) + \delta _{km}d\beta \right]$$\end{document}. Now, according to the condition for existing unique value for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}, one can write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TP(\sigma ^*-1)\le TP(\sigma ^*)\ge TP(\sigma ^*+1)$$\end{document}.

From the inequality, one can get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}-\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma -1}-pd_{max}\tau (\sigma -1)^{t-1}\le \mathbb {A}-$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma }-pd_{max}\tau \sigma ^{(t-1)}\ge \mathbb {A}-\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma +1}-pd_{max} \tau (\sigma +1)^{t-1}$$\end{document}. Simplifying this inequality, one can get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pd_{max}\tau \left[ (\sigma -1)^{t-1}-\sigma ^{t-1}\right] +\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma (\sigma -1)}\ge 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$pd_{max}\tau \left[ (\sigma +1)^{t-1}-\sigma ^{t-1}\right] -\frac{\mathbb {B}}{\sigma (\sigma +1)}\ge 0$$\end{document}. This implies that for the optimal value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _1\ge 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _2\ge 0$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Lemma 1

The optimal value of the cycle time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} exists if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{n}H_rr_{ri}}{2}+\frac{H_md(d+2\rho _m)}{2\rho _m}+\frac{h_{32}d\beta }{2} +$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t\left[ \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}\left( \frac{r_{ri}}{\sigma }\right) \right.$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. +\sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}\left( \frac{r_{ri}}{\sigma }\right) \right] +\sum _{i=1}^{n}D_vr_{ri}>\frac{h_{31}d\rho \beta }{2y_1^2}$$\end{document}.

Proof

From Eq. 51, for the real value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _3, \Theta _4$$\end{document} both grater than zero, or both less than zero. From the expression of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _3 = \sum _{i=1}^{n}G_{ri}+B_m+G_m+B_{31}y_1+B_{32}+(F_r+V_Td\beta )y_1+$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ ec_1\alpha +ec_2(1-\alpha )\right\} (TER+TEM+TE31+TE32)$$\end{document}, it is obvious that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _3>0$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha <1$$\end{document}.

Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _4= \frac{\sum _{i=1}^{n}H_rr_{ri}}{2}+\frac{H_md(d+2\rho _m)}{2\rho _m}-\frac{h_{31}d\rho \beta }{2y_1^2}+\frac{h_{32}d\beta }{2} +$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t\left[ \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}\left( \frac{r_{ri}}{\sigma }\right) +\sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}\left( \frac{r_{ri}}{\sigma }\right) \right] +\sum _{i=1}^{n}D_vr_{ri}$$\end{document} must be grater than zero. By simplifying this, one can get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\sum _{i=1}^{n}H_rr_{ri}}{2}+\frac{H_md(d+2\rho _m)}{2\rho _m}+\frac{h_{32}d\beta }{2} +$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_t\left[ \sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{im}\left( \frac{r_{ri}}{\sigma }\right) +\sum _{i=1}^{n}\delta _{ik}\left( \frac{r_{ri}}{\sigma }\right) \right] +$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i=1}^{n}D_vr_{ri}>\frac{h_{31}d\rho \beta }{2y_1^2}$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Proposition 1

The condition that the solution of production rate (Eq. 53) is positive if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_md\tau +2(1-\beta )c_1>H_m'\beta \sum _{i=1}^{n}r_{ri}$$\end{document}, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\beta <1$$\end{document}.

The first-order partial derivatives and the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _3$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _4$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _i$$\end{document} and partial derivatives are given in Appendix A and Appendix B, respectively.

Sufficient condition

Theorem 2

The sufficient condition for optimality in the classical optimization method states that all the principal minors of the Hessian matrix are alternating in sign at the optimum value of decision variables in a profit maximization problem. In another way, the Hessian matrix should be negative definite for the global optimality of the profit function. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\times 4$$\end{document} Hessian matrix is given below:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&H_{4\times 4}= \left[ \begin{array}{cccc} \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial ^2\beta } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \beta \partial \tau } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \beta \partial S_{p_i}} & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \beta \partial \rho _m}\\ \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \tau \partial \beta } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial ^2\tau } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \tau \partial S_{p_i}} & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \tau \partial \rho _m}\\ \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial S_{p_i}\partial \beta } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial S_{p_i}\partial \tau } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial ^2S_{p_i}} & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial S_{p_i}\partial \rho _m}\\ \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \rho _m\partial \beta } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \rho _m\partial \tau } & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial \rho _m\partial S_{p_i}} & \frac{\partial ^2TP}{\partial ^2 \rho _m}\\ \end{array}\right].&\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

In the proposed model, the number of distinct continuous decision variables of the profit function TP is four. Hence, the order of the Hessian matrix is four, i.e., its dimension is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\times 4$$\end{document}, and all the principal minors are alternate in sign. Numerically the values of all principal minors are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{1\times 1} = -0.00556631$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{2\times 2} = 0.0578457$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{3\times 3} = -1302.45$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{4\times 4} = 3.353\times 10^{6}$$\end{document}. The analytical proofs of the sufficient conditions are given below: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Proposition 2

All first ordered principal minors of the Hessian matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{4\times 4}$$\end{document} are negative at the unique values of the decision variables if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[ \frac{H_md^2\tau -H_m'\beta d \sum _{i=1}^{n}r_{ri}}{2}+b_1(1-\beta )d\right] >0$$\end{document}.

Proof

Appendix C states the proof of this proposition. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Proposition 3

All second ordered principal minors of the Hessian matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{4\times 4}$$\end{document} are positive at the unique values of the decision variables if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{4a\Theta _3}{\tau ^3}>\left[ \frac{V_Tdy_1}{\tau }-\left( h_{32}+\frac{h_{31}}{y_1^2}\right) \frac{d}{2}\right] ^2$$\end{document}.

Proof

Appendix C gives the proof of this proposition. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Proposition 4

All third ordered principal minors of the Hessian matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{4\times 4}$$\end{document} are negative, as values of all parameters are positive at the optimum values of all decision variables, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}_1+\frac{(H_m'\beta -H_m\tau )}{\rho _m^2(x_i+2y_iS_{p_i})}\mathbb {A}_3<\mathbb {A}_2$$\end{document}, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|H_{2\times 2}|>0$$\end{document}.

Proof

Appendix C includes the proof of it. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Proposition 5

All fourth ordered principal minors of the Hessian matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{4\times 4}$$\end{document} are positive, as values of all parameters are positive at the optimum values of all decision variables, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{\rho _m^2}\left[ \frac{H_md^2+H_m'\beta d\sum _{i=1}^{n} r_{r_i}+2b_1d}{2}\right] +\frac{V_Tdy_1}{\tau ^2}>b_2d+\left( h_{32}+\frac{h_{31}}{y_1^2}\right) +\left( \frac{H_m'\beta -H_m\tau }{2}\right) \mathbb {A}_3$$\end{document}, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|H_{3\times 3}|<0$$\end{document}.

Proof

Appendix C states the proof of the proposition. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

BSS algorithm

The proposed model is a MLNLP problem, having both integer and continuous variables. The problem is solved using the following algorithm provided as follows:

Step 1 Input all parameter’s values.

Step 2 Give the input values of selling price, cycle length, return rate , and production rate.

Step 2.1 Set initial value for container capacity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma = 1.$$\end{document}

Step 2.2 Obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^*$$\end{document} from Eq. 50.

Step 2.3 Obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^*$$\end{document} from Eq. 51.

Step 2.4 Obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_i}^*$$\end{document} from Eq. 52i = 1, 2, . . ., n.

Step 2.5 Obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m^*$$\end{document} from Eq. 53.

Step 2.6 Find the demand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_{r_i}^*.$$\end{document}

Step 2.7 Find the shipment schedule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*$$\end{document} by using following relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{r_{[i]}}{L_{[i]}}\ge \frac{r_{[i+1]}}{L_{[i+1]}}$$\end{document} 66 for all i = 1, 2, . . . ,n.

Step 2.8 If TP(i)≥TP(i+1), go to the Step 3. Else, repeat Steps 2.1-2.7.

Step 3 TP(i)* is the global maximum profit for the obtained values in Steps 2.1-2.5.

Step 4 Stop.

Numerical experiment

A numerical example is performed to establish the numerical validation of the proposed model. In other words, it provides the convergence of the formulated profit function. The values of the parameters that are used to generate the model mathematically are accessed from the literature Kim64 regarding lightning and air handling. Parametric values of building management, common services, and machine run are taken from Tandon56, and some other parametric values are taken from Sarkar et al.58 for their best fit. These values of all parameters are listed in Table 3. However, the data taken from the literature may not be the same for the convergence issue of the formulated profit function. Mathematica 11.0 software is used to obtain the optimum results in a laptop with 64-bit Windows 11 operating system, Intel i7 processor, and 8 GB of RAM. Optimization was solved with tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-6}$$\end{document} and runtime limit 600 s. The total computation time was 0.077 s. The numerical experiment began with the same data set used in the literature mentioned earlier. A synthesis of multiple subjects generates novel attributes of the function. The convergence precision varies with the function. Moreover, not all conceptions align precisely with the literature, and comprehensive data is unavailable for novel ideas. These values are estimates based on survey results. For the numerical experiment, four retailers are selected. All four retailers buy tires from a single manufacturer for sale. The prices of new tires vary across retailers due to geographic factors, as the four retailers are situated in distinct areas. The optimum profit of the formulated problem is $66,696.70, which is provided in Table 4. Special cases

This subsection contain some special cases related to this study on tire industry. All the special cases are described one after another.

Analyzing the importance of smart energy for sustainability in the CLSCM

The combination of conventional and renewable energy is called smart energy. Smart energy is reliable and economical because it utilises renewable energy. A lot of energy is used in the tire industry for production, building management, and lighting. As a result, energy prices significantly affect the system’s profit. The total profit of the system is $61,759.40 for 100% use of conventional energy and $66,696.70 for 42% use of smart energy. Therefore, the results suggest that the system will be more profitable to use smart energy rather than conventional energy. The optimal values of other variables are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m = 1,437.39$$\end{document} units/unit time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 41$$\end{document}%, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_1} = 170.12$$\end{document} ($/unit), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_2} = 164.93$$\end{document}, ($/unit), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_3} = 175.34$$\end{document} ($/unit), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_4} = 170.82$$\end{document} ($/unit), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau = 1.80$$\end{document} months. A comparison with the proposed problem is shown in Fig. 6a. Smart energy use can reduce or manage the enormous emissions produced by traditional energy. The present study indicates that 81.27 tons of carbon are emitted per cycle in a conventional energy supply chain from a tire supply chain. However, using 42% smart energy reduces emissions to 47.14 tons per cycle. Thus, the analysis shows that smart energy is profitable for the industry and the environment.

Pure production versus manufacturing-remanufacturing: A numerical insight

Pure production is the process in which the manufacturer produces finished products using only raw materials. This section includes an in-depth analysis to determine if a pure production or a hybrid (manufacturing-remanufacturing) approach is preferable. This section presents four cases for comparison with the proposed approach. The first one is pure production, i.e., remanufacturing is not considered, and in this case, the total profit is $62,226.30/cycle. The second one is that the manufacturer performed a hybrid production strategy in which the remanufacturing rate is fixed, and the total profit is $65,919.50/cycle. In the third case, the production rate is fixed, but the remanufacturing rate is variable, and in this case, the total profit is $65,877.10/cycle. The last one, where the manufacturing and remanufacturing are fixed, and the total profit is $66,207.40/cycle. The results suggest that the total profit in a hybrid approach with variable manufacturing and remanufacturing is the highest and globally optimal. The total profit in this policy is $66,696.70 per cycle. The results for the variables and total profit across all approaches are given in Table 5. As a result of the findings, an integrated strategy is more beneficial than a pure manufacturing plan. A comparative study with the original problem is attached in Fig. 6b.Table 5Optimum results of the pure and hybrid production models.ModelsReturn rate (%)Selling price ($/unit)Cycle timeProduction rate (unit/unit time)Container capacity (units)Total profit ($/cycle)Pure0172.12, 166.931.811,296.234562,226.30177.33, 172.81FixedFixed169.63, 164.451.671,360.904565,919.50remanufacturing174.85, 170.33Fixed40169.34, 164.151.69Fixed4565,877.10production174.56, 170.04BothFixed169.46, 164.281.64Fixed4566,207.40fixed174.67, 170.16

Economic sustainability under price volatility in the CLSCM

In this case, the importance of price volatility is explained. In the study, a price-dependent variable demand is established. If the price is fixed, then the scenario is defined in this case. The parametric values are similar to the original problem, only the price of products are fixed and taken as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_1}$$\end{document}=$167 per unit, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_2}$$\end{document}= $170 per unit, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_3}$$\end{document}= $165 per unit, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_4}$$\end{document}= $158 per unit. Then, the unique values of other variables are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}= 31%, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} = 45 units, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau$$\end{document} =1.62 months, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m$$\end{document} = 1360.99 unit/unit time, and STP = $64,133.20 per cycle. A comparative study is given in the Fig. 6c. The comparison indicates price volatility is always profitable rather than fixed price of the tire.

Analyzing the relation between carbon emissions and remanufacturing

Environmental and financial considerations are integral to the rapid evolution of the remanufacturing business. Emissions from transportation in the reverse supply chain, along with the per-unit cost of those emissions, are challenges remanufacturing activities face. If the manufacturer increases remanufacturing of EOL tires, the number of transportation trips to collect EOL tires from end consumers increases, and therefore the associated emissions. According to government rules, industries often take steps to increase revenue while protecting the environment. A relation between remanufacturing and carbon cost is established in Fig. 6d. The figure shows a trade-off between the unit carbon cost and the remanufacturing rate of EOL tires. The proposed model determines the optimal remanufacturing rate for a given carbon cost.

Field study: an industrial viewpoint

This section presents an industrial case study to validate the developed model robustly. The actual data volunteered by the Indian tire manufacturing industry was used to assess the reliability of the present work in a real-world setting. The company is glad to work together to complete this research work efficiently. Several parameters are adjusted as needed to improve the model’s convergence. The industry provided the values of the real data with their consent. The parametric values for this case study are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_m$$\end{document} = 350 $/setup, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_m$$\end{document} = 60 $/order, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m$$\end{document} = 6 $/unit/unit time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m'$$\end{document} = 6 $/unit/unit time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} From the industrial viewpoint, four cases were studied. All these cases are discussed one by one. The tire manufacturing industry follows a policy of not using smart energy; i.e., it uses 100% traditional energy (Case I). The overall profit of the system in Case I is $59,327.30 per cycle. Now, based on data collected from the industry, we conducted a few experiments. The first experiment with these data (Case II) included a flexible production system under smart energy consumption, but the manufacturer did not include the remanufacturing option (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 0$$\end{document}). In this case, the total profit of the system is $61,457.30 per cycle. The next one, i.e., Case III, extended the previous case with a fixed remanufacturing rate of 38%. The total profit in this case is $62,640.90 per cycle. Case IV considered fixed manufacturing and fixed remanufacturing, and the profit is $62,609.40/cycle. But, if the industry aligns with the proposed policies in this study, then the system makes a total profit of $64,225.60 per cycle.

From Table 6, one can see the best result from the case study performed using the organization’s data. Table 7 provides a comparative study of the system’s total profit across different cases in the case study. One can easily see from Table 7 that the study that considers smart energy consumption, variable demand, and a flexible production system with variable remanufacturing and variable selling price provides the best result. This is the case because, among all cases, the proposed strategy yields the maximum profit when the industry adopts a hybrid production strategy; therefore, the company was happy to accept our proposed approach.Table 6Optimum results of the field study.Decision variablesOptimal results\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{p_i}^*$$\end{document} ($/unit)170.24, 165.05, 175.44, 170.92\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ^*$$\end{document} (month)1.70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ^*$$\end{document} (rate)0.40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^*$$\end{document} (units)45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _m^*$$\end{document} (units/unit time)1354.88Total profit ($/cycle)64,225.60Table 7Profit comparison among different case studies.Case ICase IICase IIICase IVThis model59,327.30 ($/cycle)61,457.30 ($/cycle)62,640.90 ($/cycle)62,609.40 ($/cycle)64,225.60 ($/cycle)
