Discussion

This part discusses the obtained results, a comparative study with existing literature, discussion about most sensitive parameters, and some critical information for managers.

Result discussion

A manufacturing-remanufacturing-based sustainable three-echelon closed-loop TSCM is formulated to assess the value of smart energy in the presence of a manufacturer, a 3PL, and four retailers. A numerical illustration of the original problem and three specific scenarios are explored to validate the model. One of the tire business’s most essential and costly aspects is energy use for manufacturing and inventory management. The numerical results show that incorporating smart energy can boost total profit while reducing environmental impact. Based on the infrastructure and smart tools used to implement smart energy, the outcome demonstrates that implementing smart energy in part (42%) optimises overall profit by 7.40%. However, resources like coal are preserved, and emissions are reduced by 41.99% when smart energy is used instead of conventional energy. Figure 7 depicts the change in total profit and emissions when the percentage of smart energy is changed between the range 0 and 1. This result is obtained by fixing all decision variables listed in Table 4. From Fig. 7, one noticeable thing is that the rate of increase of profit declines with the rise of the rate of declining emissions due to the use of smart energy. This relation shows a clear trade-off between emissions and profit of the SCM.Fig. 7Pictorial representation of the change of profit and emissions due to he change in percentage of smart energy.

Pictorial representation of the change of profit and emissions due to he change in percentage of smart energy.

Eco-efficiency frontire basically represents a trade-off between economic performance and environmental benefits. In the context of the proposed study, it describes the trade-off between profit and emissions reduction from SCM with changes in the proportion of smart energy. Specifically, the study highlights that as the proportion of smart energy increases, the profit of the SCM increases along with reduced emissions. As both the profit and the environmental benefits enhances with the increase of smart energy share, this model describes that the proper adaptation of smart energy pushes the eco-efficiency frontier outward.

The EOL tires in the landfill discharge toxins into the air, water, and land, destroying the ecology and releasing deadly black smoke into the air whenever they catch fire (https://ecogreenequipment.com/environmental-impacts-of-waste-tire-disposal/ accessed at 14:57 IST on 14-05-2023). The remanufacturing of scrap tires reduces the detrimental environmental impact and protects natural resources. The numerical results demonstrate that a hybrid manufacturing-remanufacturing approach reduces the volume of waste tires and increases total profit by 16.44% compared to a pure manufacturing policy. However, in the proposed study, the manufacturer reduces waste by remanufacturing EOL tires. The efficiency of waste reduction is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\text{ Remanufacturing } \text{ volume }}{\text{ Total } \text{ volume } \text{ produced }}$$\end{document}. Since the remanufacturing rate is equivalent to the collection rate, the efficiency of the manufacturer in waste reduction is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\alpha Q}{Q}=\alpha =\ 41\%$$\end{document}. This discussion shows that the hybrid strategy is efficient at increasing profits by 6.70% and reducing waste by 41%. The hybrid strategy with variable remanufacturing yields the model’s global best performance.

In the proposed CLSC model, tire remanufacturing increases the number of transports and, consequently, the associated carbon emissions. The proposed model incorporates carbon-emissions prices alongside transportation costs to support sustainability goals. The carbon cost associated with transportation and the remanufacturing rate are inverse. That is, an increase in carbon cost will reduce the remanufacturing volume. The relation is presented in Fig. 6d.

The on-time supply of finished tires and the maintenance of an appropriate delivery sequence are of the utmost importance for the manufacturer and all retailers whenever RTI is used for transportation. The ideal delivery sequence is profoundly affected by store lead time. Consequently, Retailer 2, with the lowest demand, receives replenishment first because it has the shortest lead time. Retailers’ lead time is essential when using RTI for tire transportation in SCM. Otherwise, the seller’s demand chooses the most effective delivery schedule. After receiving the scheduled delivery, retailers sell tires. Retailers offer tires at variable prices to the market. Numerically, it has been found that price volatility yields 5.98% more profit than a fixed price. The proposed CLSC model supports sustainability by increasing profits through variable pricing, reducing emissions, and conserving resources through smart energy and remanufacturing.

In the study, the manufacturer delivers final products to all retailers according to a delivery schedule aligned with retailers’ lead times and demand. This approach improves the flexibility and efficiency of the proposed SCM compared to a traditional supply chain. In a traditional SCM, the manufacturer often relies on fixed schedules or bulk shipments, leading to retailers’ overstocking and increased holding costs, or delayed shipments. However, the numerical findings of this study show that if the manufacturer does not follow the delivery schedule by following retailers’ lead time, then the total profit of the SCM is $66,156.40 per cycle, which is almost 0.81% less than the profit of the proposed model. Moreover, the selling price $(169.41, 164.15, 174.50, 169.92)/unit and cycle time (1.646 months) are slightly increased, while the production rate (1382.67 units/unit time) decreased. Therefore, the numerical evaluation indicates that the proposed method is superior to the traditional one.

In this section, the proposed study is compared with the existing literature. Sarkar et al.67 did not consider carbon emissions in their model, but the current model, which studies emissions from transportation under a carbon tax policy, shows how emissions can be reduced with smart energy.

Comparison with existing literature.

Sarkar et al.58 invented a closed-loop SCM under RTI management to lessen environmental impact. In their model, they determined the optimal delivery schedule, cycle time, and lot size for retailers. They minimize the total cost of the CLSC management. While the proposed study optimizes total profit, it emphasizes the importance of smart energy in optimizing profit and reducing emissions in a tire CLSCM. Sarkar et al.58 invented a closed-loop SCM under RTI management to lessen environmental impact. In their model, they determined the optimal delivery schedule, cycle time, and lot size for retailers. They minimize the total cost of the CLSC management. While the proposed study optimizes total profit, it emphasizes the importance of smart energy in optimizing profit and reducing emissions in a tire CLSCM. Moreover, the study found that a hybrid manufacturing-remanufacturing policy is best under variable production and variable demand. Sarkar et al. Sarkar et al.58 considered carbon emissions from transportation only, but the current study shows how emissions can be reduced through smart energy.

Both Iqbal et al.11 and the proposed studies examine sustainability in supply networks; however, they differ in focus, structure, and environmental strategies. Iqbal et al.11 advocates a zero-waste strategy for green supply chain management, proposing a three-tier primary–secondary–closed-loop system designed to eliminate waste by transforming spoiled food into secondary products and recycling packaging materials. Along with these, they aimed to reduce energy use through optimal cycle times. But the proposed model, smart energy–enabled three-echelon CLSCM for tires, focuses on minimizing carbon emissions and enhancing profitability by integrating smart energy, price-dependent demand, carbon tax regulations, and variable manufacturing-remanufacturing. Iqbal et al.11 focuses on waste reduction and energy efficiency in food supply chains. In contrast, this study underscores the importance of emission reduction, smart energy utilization, and profitability in tire manufacturing, illustrating complementary yet distinct approaches to sustainable supply chain management.

Both Iqbal et al.11 and the proposed studies examine sustainability in supply networks; however, they differ in focus, structure, and environmental strategies. Iqbal et al.11 advocates a zero-waste strategy for green supply chain management, proposing a three-tier primary–secondary–closed-loop system designed to eliminate waste by transforming spoiled food into secondary products and recycling packaging materials. Along with these, they aimed to reduce energy use through optimal cycle times. But the proposed model, smart energy–enabled three-echelon CLSCM for tires, focuses on minimizing carbon emissions and enhancing profitability by integrating smart energy, price-dependent demand, carbon tax regulations, and variable manufacturing-remanufacturing. Iqbal et al.11 focuses on waste reduction and energy efficiency in food supply chains. In contrast, this study underscores the importance of emission reduction, smart energy utilization, and profitability in tire manufacturing, illustrating complementary yet distinct approaches to sustainable supply chain management.

Sarkar et al.67 developed a smart sustainable production model for multi-type bio-fuel under a flexible production system, inspection, and price-dependent variable demand. They had used renewable energy for air handling and lighting in the manufacturing sector for inventory management. While the current model is developed with a three-echelon supply chain for tires, this study examines the importance of smart energy in production for manufacturers and in inventory management for each player. Moreover, the study found that a hybrid manufacturing-remanufacturing policy is best under variable production, variable demand, and optimal delivery scheduling. Sarkar et al.67 did not consider carbon emissions in their model, but the current model, which studies emissions from transportation under a carbon tax policy, shows how emissions can be reduced with smart energy.

Sensitivity analysis

This section describes the sensitivity analysis of some crucial parameters. The performance of the sensitivity analysis helps understand the robustness of the proposed model and how changes in input parameters affect the total profit of the formulated profit function. The proposed model assesses sensitivity to changes in input parameters of ±50% and ±25%. Analyzing different parameters is very helpful for business organizations in making informed decisions on the use of smart energy, flexible production, inventory management, pricing, and other effective choices. However, the sensitivity of the three players’ crucial parameters is shown below, and the observations are discussed.

Sensitivity analysis for retailers’ parameters

In this part of the sensitivity analysis, some crucial parameter of the retailer are analyzed to revel their impact on the total profit of the system (Fig. 8).

Analysis of demand parameters

Demand forecasting for any SCM is very important. Accurate forecasting can allow the retailer to manage inventory levels within its capacity, avoiding overstock or stockouts. Demand changes for various reasons; price is one of them. Further, the initial market demand parameter is very sensitive to profit maximization. Now, a 50% decrease in initial demand results in a 28.68% reduction in total profit and an increase in the same influence of total profit by 28.68%. For the change of the parameter by ±25%, the total profit changes by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+$$\end{document}10.71% and −10.72%, respectively (Fig. 8a). The price transformation factor in the demand function is another sensitive parameter for changes in total profit. The modification in this factor (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i$$\end{document}) by ±50% implies that the total profit changes −2.13% and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+$$\end{document}2.13%, respectively (Fig. 8b).

Analysis of holding cost and lead time parameter

The retailer stores products after receiving them from the manufacturer and keeps them until all products are sold out. In that case, if the unit holding cost changes drastically, then the overall expenses and hence the profit will be affected by this. For this model, it was found that if the unit holding cost rises by 50%, the profit decreases to 5.81%, and a 50% decrease in unit holding cost results in an increase in total profit of 5.82% (Fig. 8b). The other crucial parameter is the retailer’s lead time. If the lead time, i.e., the time between placing an order and receiving it, is increased, then demand may decrease and hence profit. In this model, it was found that if the lead time increased by 50%, the total profit decreased by 0.37%, and decreasing the lead time by 50% increased the total profit by 0.37% (Fig. 8d).

Analysis of ordering cost and energy consumption related parameter

This part of the sensitivity analysis discusses the effects of the ordering cost factor and the energy consumption parameter for light and air handling. For the change in the retailer’s ordering cost by ±50%, the total profit of the supply chain changes symmetrically by ±0.05%. Again, if one changes the retailer’s lighting energy consumption parameter by ±50%, the SCM profit changes by ±0.02%. Whereas, for the change in the energy consumption parameter of the air handling by ±50% and ±25%, the supply chain profit changes by ±0.12% and ±0.05%, respectively. A graph for the same can be found in Fig. 8c.Fig. 8Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis of different parameter of retailers.

Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis of different parameter of retailers.

Sensitivity analysis for the manufacturer’s parameters

This part analyzes the manufacturer’s parameters to show their sensitivity to the total profit of the supply chain. The graphical representation is given in Fig. 9

Analysis of energy related parameter

In any production system, one of the most crucial requirements is energy for tasks such as machine operation, building management, air handling, common services, and others. Now, the change in energy consumption across tasks has a different impact on total profit. Like energy consumption, the price per unit of energy has a crucial effect on total profit as well. The analysis found that the energy consumption for production is the most sensitive parameter. Numerically, it was observed that if consumption increased by 50%, the total profit decreased by 12.75%, and if consumption decreased by 50%, the total profit increased by 15.55% (Fig. 9e). If the consumption for lighting, BMS, and common services changes by ±50%, the total profit changes almost the same in both directions, which are almost 2% in each case (Fig. 9d). The other crucial parameter is the price of renewable energy per unit. The decrease in renewable energy cost by 50% shows an increase in the profit by 3.45%, and an increase of per unit cost declines the profit by 3.63% (Fig. 9b).

Analysis of production related parameter

In an SCM, unit production cost and an efficient manufacturing setup with a variable production rate significantly affect SCM profit. The variability in the manufacturing setup can respond to changing market demand by adjusting the manufacturing rate. The unit manufacturing cost under the variable production system varies with raw material costs, development, and tool/die costs. The analysis shows that profit decreased by 24.77% when raw material costs increased by 50% and raw material expenses decreased by 50%, resulting in a 26.94% increase in total profit. For the change in raw material expenses by ±25%, the total profit changes by −12.66% and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+$$\end{document}3.20%, respectively (Fig. 9f). The next sensitive parameter related to production is the tool/die cost. The reduction of this cost by 50% implies the increase of the total profit by 4.26%, and the profit decreased to 3.19% when the value of the tool/die component increased by 50% (Fig. 9b). The development cost parameter is another sensitive parameter. The profit decreases by 1.02% and increases by 1.05% for the change by 50% and −50% (see Fig. 9a). The remanufacturing rate for remanufactured EOL product is variable and equivalent to the collection rate. Now, the change in remanufacturing costs has a crucial impact on the total profit of the SCM. Analysis found that due to the change in remanufacturing cost by ±50%, the total profit changed by −3.57% and 3.62%, respectively (Fig. 9b).

Analysis of holding cost related parameter

In the proposed model, the manufacturer remanufactures EOL products along with the production of new products. Therefore, the manufacturer needs to store both new products and EOL products. In that case, a change in unit product holding cost may significantly affect total profit. The analysis found that, when the per-unit holding cost of both EOL and new products increased by 50%, the total profit decreased to 1.02% and 9.55%, respectively. On the other hand, if the per-unit holding cost decreases by 50%, the total profit increases by 1.01% and 11.72%, respectively. The graphical representations for both analyses are depicted in Figs. 9a, e.

Analysis of ordering and setup cost related parameter

In the manufacturer’s production process within an SCM, setting up a smooth, effective production system is crucial. In that case, the setup cost parameter is very effective for a profitable SCM. An increase in this parameter by 50% reduces the total profit by 0.17%, whereas a 50% decrease in this cost parameter implies an increase in the total profit by 0.17% (Fig. 9c). Therefore, the setup cost parameter is highly sensitive, and supply chain managers should carefully determine setup costs. Ordering expenses for the retailer are another essential factor with a significant impact on supply chain profitability. The analysis shows that if the ordering cost is reduced by 50%, the total profit increases to 0.03%, and if the value of the ordering cost is increased by 50%, the total profit increases to 0.03% (Fig. 9c).Fig. 9Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis of different crucial parameters of the manufacturer.

Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis of different crucial parameters of the manufacturer.

Sensitivity analysis for 3PLs parameter and smart energy parameter

The variation in total profit for the change in some key parameter of 3PLs are analyzed here. The graphical representation is given in Fig. 10

Analysis of smart energy parameter

The model is developed to assess the efficiency of smart energy, a combination of traditional and renewable energy. Because the prices per unit of traditional and renewable energy differ, the change in the percentage of renewable energy has a significant impact on total profit. The model found that 42% use of smart energy provided the optimal result. Now, if all players of the supply chain use 50% less smart energy, then the total profit decreases to 3.45% and the increase of the same by 50% leads the total profit to 3.63% (Fig. 10d).

Analysis of holding and collection cost parameter

During the collection of EOL products, 3PL incurs per-unit collection costs, and after collection, 3PL stores them before sending them to the manufacturer. If end consumers demand more to sell EOL tires, how does that affect the system’s profit? The analysis revealed that a 50% increase in collection costs leads to a total profit of −2.86%, and if the 3PL manages to pay 50% to end consumers, the total profit rises to 2.89% (Fig. 10a). The other sensitive parameter is the per-unit holding cost of EOL products. The results showed that a 50% change in unit holding cost leads to a 1.46% change in total profit (Fig. 10a).

Analysis of transportation and emissions related parameter

In the proposed CLSCM for tires, the involved 3PLs collect EOL tires, transport them to the manufacturer, and provide the manufacturer with a container for delivering final products to all retailers. Throughout this process, transportation is handled by 3PLs. The increase in transportation leads to increased emissions, which implies an increase in total cost68. The sensitivity analysis found that, with fixed and variable transportation costs rising by 50%, total profit decreases by 0.37% and 0.06%, respectively. The decrease of the same by 50% argues that the total profit increases by 0.38% and 0.06% respectively. Like transportation cost, the change in variable emissions cost by ±50%, the total profit changes symmetrically by 0.71%. Graphically, these are represented in Figs. 10b, c.Fig. 10Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis of 3PL and the use of smart energy consumption.

Graphical representation of sensitivity analysis of 3PL and the use of smart energy consumption.

The model is developed with approximately 120 parameters, but not all of them have a significant impact on the study’s outcome. Some parameters, such as the ith retailer’s order quantity (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_i$$\end{document}) and the manufacturer’s lot size (q), are dependent variables. Furthermore, the parameters used for energy consumption due to the use of fan, light, machine, BMS, hours of lighting usage, and common services, and the parameters used for the number of bulbs and fans used by the retailers, manufacturer, and 3PLs and the distance between nodes of the CLSCM actually these parameters ensure structural completeness of the mathematical model and these parameters have negligible impact on the outcome of the proposed study.

In addition to these parameters, after thorough investigation of the model through sensitivity analysis, we categorised the parameters into two groups: those with a strong influence on the outcome and those with moderate influence. The parameters with strong influence are the proportion of smart energy used (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}), the price of renewable and traditional energy (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ec_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ec_2$$\end{document}), raw material cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_0$$\end{document}), development cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_1$$\end{document}), tool/die cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_2$$\end{document}), initial demand (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _i$$\end{document}), scalling parameter of price (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_f,~ D_v,~D_{vr},~D_{fr}$$\end{document}) parameters.

The parameters with moderate influence on the outcome are the setup cost and holding cost for all players involved in the CLSCM, transportation cost, and the scale parameter related to container management.

Computational complexity of the problem

The proposed model is developed with a manufacturer, 3PL, and multiple retailers to maximize the overall profit. The developed profit function is a mixed integer non linear function. The computational complexity of the formulated problem increases non polynomially with the increases of the number of the decision variables. In the model the number of continuous decision variables is moderate, which are selling price, production rate, collection rate, and cycle time and one integer variable, which is container capacity. Therefore the computational complexity is manageable for medium and large size systems. Furthermore the convergence of the formulated function is achieved using Mathematica 11.0 software.

The scalability of the model is tested through the discussion of the sensitivity analysis of the profit function for different parameter used to formulate the model. The sensitivity was performed with a range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-50$$\end{document}% to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+50$$\end{document}% with a step size of 25%. The sensitivity analysis discusses the stable convergence of the model. For the larger industrial system which involves many retailer or multiple products, the computational duration may increase with the increase of retailers. However the growth of this time complexity remains tractable for large size systems like involving 10 to 20 retailers under modern computational platforms.

Managerial insights

This section provides some points for the industry managers by analyzing the result of this extensive research. Energy is one of the backbones of production and inventory management in the tire SCM. As a result, it is an essential responsibility for the manager to maximize profits through effective energy management. Smart energy is cost-effective and ecologically beneficial since it combines traditional and renewable energy. As a result, for smart energy consumption following the proposed policy, profit increased by up to 12.7%, and emissions were reduced by up to 41.98%. Therefore, managers should follow the proposed policy to maximize total profits and minimize emissions.Following numerical research, the energy cost for lighting and air handling significantly impacts the system’s profit. Reducing costs in these two scenarios is undoubtedly an essential challenge for managers. In building management services like air conditioning, heating, ventilation, and lighting, the introduction of automation can reduce energy consumption by up to 25% to 50% and hence the total cost regarding energy consumption69. Therefore, installing a motion sensor can automate lighting and air conditioning systems. Automating lighting and air conditioning systems may reduce energy consumption costs while increasing profit. Consequently, tire supply chain management managers should automate lighting and air conditioning systems by installing motion sensors.SCM managers have a dilemma when establishing a suitable production strategy. The numerical findings of the presented model show that a hybrid manufacturing-remanufacturing method reduces the volume of waste tires and increases overall profit more than a pure manufacturing policy. The hybrid strategy has two options: fixed remanufacturing and variable remanufacturing. The hybrid strategy with variable remanufacturing yields the model’s global best performance. The SCM managers should follow the proposed model to maximize their profit.The discussion of the results of the proposed model indicates that there are several scopes for the Governments to implement policies which can accelerate the transition toward sustainable and energy-efficient closed-loop supply chain. The Government may introduce subsidy policy for the use of renewable energy, which can be economically attractive for the SCM players in increasing the percentage of renewable energy share in the smart energy. Along with the policy makers can increase the investment in the improvement of smart grid and digital infrastructure, which may effectively reduce the operational barriers and enhance the reliability of smart energy integration for the SCM players. Further the Government or the policy makers can provide the incentives to support the green transportation system such as electric fleet adoption, fuel-efficient routing technologies. These green initiatives can effective in reducing emissions in both forward and reverse SCM from the transportation sections.The optimum delivery sequence for a store indicates the significance of the retailer’s lead time. Consequently, Retailer 2, with the lowest demand, gets a replenishment first since it has the shortest lead time. Retailers’ lead time is essential when using returnable transport items (RTI) for tire transportation in SCM. Otherwise, the seller’s demand chooses the most effective delivery schedule. However, while using RTI, managers must consider retailer demand and lead time.

SCM managers have a dilemma when establishing a suitable production strategy. The numerical findings of the presented model show that a hybrid manufacturing-remanufacturing method reduces the volume of waste tires and increases overall profit more than a pure manufacturing policy. The hybrid strategy has two options: fixed remanufacturing and variable remanufacturing. The hybrid strategy with variable remanufacturing yields the model’s global best performance. The SCM managers should follow the proposed model to maximize their profit.

The discussion of the results of the proposed model indicates that there are several scopes for the Governments to implement policies which can accelerate the transition toward sustainable and energy-efficient closed-loop supply chain. The Government may introduce subsidy policy for the use of renewable energy, which can be economically attractive for the SCM players in increasing the percentage of renewable energy share in the smart energy. Along with the policy makers can increase the investment in the improvement of smart grid and digital infrastructure, which may effectively reduce the operational barriers and enhance the reliability of smart energy integration for the SCM players. Further the Government or the policy makers can provide the incentives to support the green transportation system such as electric fleet adoption, fuel-efficient routing technologies. These green initiatives can effective in reducing emissions in both forward and reverse SCM from the transportation sections.

Further the Government or the policy makers can provide the incentives to support the green transportation system such as electric fleet adoption, fuel-efficient routing technologies. These green initiatives can effective in reducing emissions in both forward and reverse SCM from the transportation sections.

The optimum delivery sequence for a store indicates the significance of the retailer’s lead time. Consequently, Retailer 2, with the lowest demand, gets a replenishment first since it has the shortest lead time. Retailers’ lead time is essential when using returnable transport items (RTI) for tire transportation in SCM. Otherwise, the seller’s demand chooses the most effective delivery schedule. However, while using RTI, managers must consider retailer demand and lead time.

Conclusions

The proposed model studied a sustainable three-echelon CLSCM for tires with multi-retailers, single manufacturers, and 3PLs under a flexible manufacturing-remanufacturing policy. The study found the integrated effect of smart energy and carbon emissions in the proposed CLSCM for tires. The study found the maximum profit under the retailer’s price-dependent variable-demand strategy. The profit function of the CLSCM was highly nonlinear in selling price, production rate, cycle time, and EOL product collection rate. The model was solved using the classical optimization method. The numerical experiment found the following crucial results: the strategy of using smart energy by supply chain players increases total profit by 7.40% and reduces energy-related emissions by 41.99%. The analysis of the results showed that the manufacturer’s delivery scheduling strategy for final products is more cost-effective than a traditional delivery process. The proposed supply chain effectively reduced waste through the manufacturer’s remanufacturing strategy, and the results showed the manufacturer’s efficiency in waste reduction was 41%. The other interesting finding regarding remanufacturing was that there is a trade-off between remanufacturing and per unit emissions costs, i.e., as carbon costs increase, the remanufacturing rate decreases. From the retailer’s perspective, the flexible selling price strategy yielded a 3.84% increase over a fixed price policy. However, sustainability was obtained by the use of smart energy, by reducing waste under flexible manufacturing-remanufacturing procedures, and by maximizing the total system profit under a flexible price policy.

This study covers a comprehensive analysis of a three-echelon sustainable SCM by considering the effective utilization of smart energy. However, there are several areas where the study could contribute by addressing the limitations of the proposed study. The study maximizes the total profit by addressing the environmental sustainability through the use of smart energy, remanufacturing, and emissions reduction, which made the problem a single objective function based optimization model. The study is restricted to a single-objective profit-maximization framework. As a result, it does not explicitly address trade-offs among profit maximization, emissions minimization, and optimal energy consumption. This fact limits the applicability of the proposed study, as it does not address multiple sustainability criteria simultaneously while maximizing total profit. Therefore, the model could be extended by incorporating multi-objective optimization techniques to minimize emissions and energy consumption and maximize total profit of the system67. The consideration of multiple retailers made the proposed model realistic, but in the modern era, the expansion of the retail system has created a more competitive business environment. Considering only the offline retail channel limits the study’s scope for retail opportunities. In this regard, the adoption of omnichannel retailing could increase tire demand and thereby enhance the economic sustainability of the system70. In the formulation of an SCM model, each node of the SCM imposes some constraints, such as capacity and budget constraints. Further, the model discussed the emissions dimension under the carbon tax policy. But the consideration of a carbon cap-and-trade policy may be more useful in real-world scenarios for reducing environmental degradation. The absence of these constraints in the model limits its applicability in the real world. Therefore, the model could be extended to account for space and budget constraints, as well as a carbon cap constraint, to form a bounded optimization model71,72. All the parameters in the model are considered to have precise value, which is not possible in real life scenario. Therefor the model can be extended by incorporating fuzzy costs instead of crisp value only73,74.
